EPILOGUE
world only; its prescriptions did not comprehend the Mestizo society that was
coming to absorb the upper class. While Kartini and her sisters might not enter
their own society before marriage, they were pennitted to meet Dutch people,
those men as well as women whose social position in the colonial elite gave
them access to the kabupaten. They were also pennitted to leave the kabupaten
on several occasions to attend special events in the colonial world. They at­
tended the dedication of a church, for instance, and a perfonnance by a Dutch
orator in a Semarang theater. They appeared in 1898 at a ball honoring the
coronation of Holland's Queen Wilhelmina, and as recognition of their becom­
ing spokeswomen for Western education for Javanese girls of their class, Kar­
tini and her sisters were invited to spend a week meeting officials in Batavia and
Bogor to discuss their own further study.
In addition to these fonnal contacts, Kartini maintained the acquaintance of
several Dutch people through private meetings and through a large correspon­
dence. The experience of intellectual exchange as between equals, as opposed
to the ritual contacts of colonial society, was a powerful influence in shaping
the ideas for which Kartini was to become famous: ideas concerning polygyny,
marriage practices, divorce, vocational and professional training for Javanese
women and men, advocacy of Dutch as the lingua franca, revival of Javanese
arts and crafts, reform of administrative practices in promotion and preroga­
tives, changes in the role and status of women at all social levels, removal of
race and color prejudice, and association between high-ranking Dutch and
Javanese to raise the standard of living of the rural majority of Java.
Kartini 's reflections on colonial society, radical and original for her class and
time, were expressed in many forums, including meetings and correspondence
with such Dutchmen as J. H. Abendanon, director of education, cottage indus­
try, and religion from 1899 to 1905, and H. H. van Kol, a member of the
Netherlands Lower House. There were also her occasional essays printed in
Indies magazines (Yogyakarta's de Echo, for instance), in the Bijdragen tot de
Taal-, Land- en Volkenkunde (Contributions to Linguistic, Geographical, and
Anthropological Studies), then published in The Hague, in The Dutch Lily, a
feminist journal published in the Netherlands, and in a memorial to the Dutch
government. II The best-known expression of her thoughts is contained in the
collection of her letters to Dutch friends first printed in the Netherlands in
1911. 12
W. F. Wertheim has said that in 1911 Kartini exploded on a colonial Dutch
public that was totally unprepared for a Javanese woman using a fluent Dutch to
argue for reforms in Indies society.13 But she was already a well-known,
controversial figure on Java in her own lifetime, at least in circles reading
Dutch-language newspapers. The Java Bode, for instance, printed a long article
on Kartini and her sisters Roekmini and Kardinah in 1904. A hint of the quality
of Dutch approval for Indonesians who adopted their learning and ways can be
taken from this same article's description of the teaching methods employed in
165

--- PAGE: 198 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.198) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
the school for Javanese girls which the sisters had established in 1903. Kartini's
sisters, the reader is infonned, squatted down, Javanese style, beside their
pupils. "They want European culture and learning, but they remain Javanese.
. . . They are one with their own people! A lesson for many." 14
Nevertheless, the adoption of Dutch manners, the habit of speaking Dutch,
and the growing acquaintance with Dutch learning on the part of the Javanese
aristocracy drew hostility and opposition from some quarters in the immigrant
community. P. A. A. Djajadiningrat, who was one of the first Indonesians to
complete his secondary education in a school for Europeans in Batavia, has left
in his Memoirs 15 a record of slights and prejudices experienced as members of
his class mixed more with the Dutch. In 1896, the year he entered the senior
grade of primary school in Batavia, attendance by an Indonesian was still quite
new. The school's headmaster, fearing opposition from parents of the pupils,
devised a solution peculiar to the society we have been studying: Achmad
Djajadiningrat, scion of an old and honorable Sundanese family, could only be
acceptable in the guise of an illegitimate but acknowledged Eurasian, for that is
the implication of the name under which he was enrolled, Willem van Bantam
(William of [or from] Bantam). Kartini encountered race prejudice too, in the
Japara school as well as in the drawing rooms of Europeans, and spoke of it
often in her letters. 16 The experience led her to stress the need in the colony's
schools for moral training and character development, along with regular sub­
ject matter.
Stages in the adoption of Dutch practices can be followed through a perusal
of announcements concerning their domestic affairs placed by high-ranking
Javanese. It was already transgressing Javanese propriety to bring one's wife
forward for public notice, as instanced in the following advertisement, even
though the lady's own names are not disclosed: "Given birth to two Daughters:
the Wife of Radhen Toemenggoeng Danoe Koesoemo, Regent of Malang"
(1866). Established decorum was again renounced in the desire to conform to
Dutch practice in this announcement: "On the 17th of this month died sudden­
ly, after giving birth to a healthy daughter, my beloved Wife, the Raden Ajoe.
Hadiningrat, Regent of Demak" (1885). In time, women's names came to be
included in the personals placed by their male kin. For example, a death notice
is inserted in 1904 for "my beloved mother, the Raden Ajoe Pangeran Hario
Gondosiwojo," and the age of the deceased is also given. The next step was for
announcements to be issued jointly by husband and wife. In heavy type comes,
"The Regent of Toemenggoeng, R. M. T. Tjokro Adi Koesoemo and Raden
Ajoe announce the birth of a daughter" (1904). In a later issue, the Regent of
Magelang and Raden Ayu announce the approaching marriage of their daugh­
ter, whom they mark by name, Raden Ajeng Patemah. And finally, in announc­
ing the death of Kartini, her parents described her by both her marriage title and
the single names under which she had become a public figure: "The under-
166

--- PAGE: 199 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.199) ---
EPILOGUE
signed beg to express their thanks for the great sympathy they have been shown
on the loss of their daughter, R. Ajoe Adipatie Djojo Adiningrat (Raden Ajeng
Kartini), Sosroningrat and Raden Ajoe, Japara" (1904).17
Bupatis' families also adopted the habit of sending good wishes for the New
Year to the public at large, their names joining those of local notables of the
European and Chinese communities on the front page of regional newspapers.
They began setting reception days, which they also announced through their
daily papers. The rooms into which European guests were conducted were
furnished as Mestizo parlors, showing a combination of elements: heavily
carved Javanese furniture and screens; velvet draperies and carpets from
Europe; stuffed animals; collections of Javanese krises (daggers) alongside
Chinese porcelain; Japanese lacquer work; and portraits of members of the
House of Orange on the walls. 18
In illustration of increasing social exchange between the upper classes of
European and Javanese communities is the solicitation of the local bupati or the
sultan and their consorts as patrons of clubs for the Dutch. There were also the
contributions from the Indonesian side to Dutch charitable causes. For instance,
a fancy fair in aid of impoverished Eurasians was organized by the bupati of
Semarang in Augltst 1904. A string orchestra played pieces by Verdi and
Rubinstein. The players, according to the Locomotief, were all members of the
royal family of Solo and proved that Indonesians had aptitude for "the musical
conceptions of European composers. " 19 This local report suggests the extent
and scope of deliberate borrowing on the part of Java's upper classes. Such
borrowing from the customs of the colonials made them appear to be like their
counterparts in Eurasian society.
Opposition to these trends, and anxiety, was the response of Europeans
concerned about "white status." They had always expressed themselves
through the newspapers on the need to inculcate the virtue of hard work in
Javanese laborers, whom they predictably saw as slothful. When it came to the
upper classes, Dutch responses took a different tum. They knew enough of
Javanese social codes and etiquette to introduce a variation of their own: ad­
dressing a Western-educated Javanese in Dutch, but pennitting responses only
in Malay or High Javanese. In the matter of social contacts, it was natural that
such enemies of the Javanese should first object to encounters between Indo­
nesian men and European women and blame the women for "uppityness" in
Javanese men. From the many examples in the papers, one must suffice.
It concerned editorial discussion of a letter criticizing European ladies for
accompanying their husbands to the rooms of Solo's Habiprojo, a club for
Javanese government servants who were, in 1904, all men. One female guest
had, on a later occasion, been "accosted" by an Indonesian whom she had met
at the club and who had offered to escort her, this frightful exchange taking
place in the language of the colonial rulers. In concluding remarks, the editor
167

--- PAGE: 200 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.200) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
urged that serious thought be given to the question whether the prestige of
European ladies was affected by their transgressing the rules of propriety gov­
erning the behavior of aristocratic Indonesian women.20
This incident is noteworthy for another reason too. Not only does it show an
apprehension concerning interracial relations and European "prestige"; it also
illustrates the function of the waning Mestizo culture as a middle, mediating
arena between European and Indonesian. It will be remembered that at the turn
of the century over three-quarters of the Europeans on Java had been born
there, and that locally born women with European status still far outnumbered
female immigrants. Consequently the wives of many officials were Creoles or
part- Indonesians who in their early years had been raised almost exclusively by
Indonesians. What this meant for colonial society was that receptions in 1900
were still segregated between the sexes. Ladies sat together in an inner parlor,
the men outside in the front gallery. This was the way a proper Eurasian woman
was raised and the way she conducted parties as mistress of a household. Thus
when upper-class Javanese women became customary guests in the houses of
their European counterparts, the break with traditional seclusion was already
cushioned. They were not left in a drawing room crowded with men after
formal greetings had been exchanged, but rather led to a room with women who
sat, as in any kabupaten, according to their husbands' rank. Mestizo codes also
allowed for distinct entertainments in different areas for the various groups of
guests. For Europeans there would be champagne and a waltz band in a pavi­
lion isolated from the sitting rooms, and elsewhere a gamelan would play and
guests would arrange themselves before and behind the screen of the puppet
theater according to rank and sex.21
Mestizo manners had ceased to dictate public behavior by the second and
third decades of the twentieth century. By that time immigrants had clustered
in cities in sufficient numbers to order their lives as nearly as they could in the
Dutch manner. Furthermore, while the number of immigrant men and women
remained small in proportion to the total European community in the Indies,
yet they dominated the upper levels of government and society and they set the
tastes and fashions for the rest.
The Indonesian upper classes were left to reconsider the experience of
the preceding few generations. Whilst superficially they appeared most like
wealthy Indies Dutch, they too were withdrawing into their own society and
concentrating on defining an "Indonesian" identity. For the bupatis like Djaja­
diningrat, this was accomplished through the Regentenbond and through a
campaign to impose adherence to the principle of sons succeeding their fathers
in the office of bupati. The debates of the Regentenbond were conducted in
Dutch and were predicated on a continuing association with the Netherlands
within a commonwealth. By contrast, out of the debates of the youth associa­
tions representing the lower ranks of the Javanese aristocracy and young people
168

--- PAGE: 201 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.201) ---
EPILOGUE
of Indonesia's other ethnic groups there developed the goal of a state indepen­
dent of any ties to the Dutch. In 1928 they made the seemingly perverse choice
of Malay, pidgin of the VOC and tongue used to degrade, as the language of the
new nation they aspired to form. Malay was, at the same time, becoming the
language of a new, Indonesian, colonial literature that was as single-mindedly
devoted to reform as any Dutch-language Indies novel of our women writers.
Only the problems were different. Where Dutch female authors spoke of
abandoned children and concubines, Indonesian authors wrote of the alienating
effects of Western education and how to overcome them. They wrote of con­
flicts between the behavior expected by Indonesian customary law and Islam
and the manners acquired from Dutch schools and European friends. There
were the evils of polygyny and child marriage to be combatted through the
novel, and the sensitive subject of cross-racial marriage as well as of marriage
between Indonesians of widely separated social classes and different ethnic
groups. 22
All these issues were debated in the 1920s and '30s. Parallel developments in
the political arena can be briefly related. By 1908 an association of distin­
guished, Western-educated Javanese had been formed. Under the name Budi
Utom0 , which is often translated as ~ 'Glorious Endeavor," they sought a re­
vival of Javanese culture, but-and this is an instance of seeming "Mestizo­
ness' ,- through a society run on European rules with committee chairmen and
the rest. Within the next few years, others joined together in similarly organ­
ized bodies, taking as the key to their band their common ethnic or religious
identity. By 1922, some were ready to merge the local and regional for an
association based on an Indonesia-wide identity. Simultaneously there was a
movement winning adherents primarily among Eurasians. With several prom­
inent Indonesians, they joined in an "Indies homeland" party. That aspiration
was defeated, and the several communities withdrew into parties representing
the chief social and political divisions of the colony in the 1930s: Indonesian
nationalist parties, aiming for political independence; Eurasian parties, which
would give leadership of an independent Indies to the part-European; the parties
of immigrant Europeans that in the late 1930s were fascist and based on race
supremacy; and organizations for two groups omitted from discussion here, the
immigrant Chinese and the Indonesian Chinese. (The latter was another "Mes­
tizo" group that was known under the Indonesian term peranakan, or mixed
race.)
Membership in all these groups apparently became more fixed and the plural
aspects of colonial society more fully developed in the 1930s. In evidence are
the separate pay scales, occupation slots, law codes, and education systems.
All the same, there was "passing" from one community to the other. In the
case of movement into Asian groups there are no figures. The passage across
race lines into the European group is, however, open for inspection.
169

--- PAGE: 202 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.202) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
It remained exceedingly mixed. The Constitutional Law of 1818 had in­
cluded in the European category all Christian Indonesians who had joined the
European group socially. By 1854, however, a new regulation specifically
excluded Christian Indonesians from enjoying privileges reserved for the
European community. Henceforth they would be counted among the indigen­
ous Indonesian population. 23 After 1871 individual Asians could apply for
European status. This was conferred by administrative decree on those who
were Christian, used Dutch as their daily language, dressed as Europeans, and
were "Dutch" in manners and occupation. 24 Finally, in 1886, all who lived as
"Europeans" and were regarded as such by their associates were confirmed in
that status. 25
The extremely mixed nature of the group with European status, persisting
during the period when colonial society was apparently the most plural, is best
illustrated by figures collected by A. van MarIe. In explaining the increase in
the European group over the period 1881 to 1940 from causes other than
immigation, he presents the following numbers: legal assimilation (gelijkstel­
ling), 16,500; mixed marriage (for example, counting an Asian bride as Euro­
pean), 16,000; offspring of mixed marriages, 29,000; recognition of illegiti­
mate, part-Asian children, 48,000; and Creoles, 210,000. 26
The Japanese occupation of the Indonesian archipelago (1942-45) made all
that meaningless. Henceforth there were only Indonesians, Asians born outside
the archipelago, and Europeans. Europeans were interned for most of the
Pacific war, their number including all those Eurasians who remained attached
to their special status as Europeans. World war was followed by a national
revolution whose outcome, in 1949, was an Indonesian republic independent of
political ties to the Netherlands. Once again Mestizos were faced with a choice:
to apply for citizenship of Indonesia and accept the Indonesian side of their
heritage or to take up a new future overseas. Many persisted in preferring
identity as Dutch, and they scattered across the world, principally to Holland
and the United States. Another group had made a brief experiment in founding
a Eurasian homeland in West New Guinea, one that had ended, for all practical
purposes, with the Second World War. It ended as a dream when West New
Guinea was incorporated in the Republic of Indonesia in 1963, first as Irian
Barat and later under the name Irian Jaya. One other Mestizo society in the
archipelago that had been cut off for three centuries from the Dutch through
Portuguese rule, and that had developed its own distinctive culture, is now
being absorbed into Indonesia as the twenty-seventh province, Timor Timur.
Thus ended Mestizo society. Its special character as a fusion of East and
West under the status of European is no more; its legacy remains as one element
in the national and regional cultures of Indonesia.
We have now traced the social life of the Dutch, from earliest days in port
170

--- PAGE: 203 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.203) ---
EPILOGUE
cities on the coasts of Asia, through development of settled community life in
enclaves with a distinctive culture, to a period characterized by direct rule over
a territorial empire stretching out to the limits of the Indonesian archipelago.
We have watched a condition of isolation and uncertainty be replaced by a
network of communities from southern Africa to Japan whose members were
linked by marriage and position in the VOC hierarchy and whose daily life was
imbued with Asian practices. In tum, we have seen this network dismembered,
and a new separation between European and Asian worlds develop in the
nineteenth century, based on government policies of plural development and
influenced by technological advances and demographic changes in the makeup
of the dominant immigrant group.
In the earliest days, the Dutch struggled to establish for themselves a niche in
the ports along the Asian trade routes. Their arrival caused no upheaval in the
cultural life of the Asian states; they were probably regarded by the royal court
of Matararn as one more troublesome and vulgar species of tribute-bearer. Life
in the early settlements may be characterized as tumultuous: bachelor commu­
nities cut off from their homeland by the vast distances of sea, menaced by
tropical diseases whose remedies were not yet known, and vulnerable to attack
from Asian states and their European allies who were hostile to Dutch econom­
ic aims. Attitudes to Asian peoples in this period were of the coarsest kind.
Unions with Asian women, in the absence of a normal proportion of women
among the European immigrants and in the absence of the institutions of civic
life, were disorderly and exploitative.
The impulse to transfonn adventurers into citizens very quickly asserted
itself, and the development of municipal life took place within walled, fortified
cities. Attempts at colonization by sending out batches of families and or­
phaned girls or giving pennission to those with rank in the merchant hierarchy
of the Company to bring out their wives and other female relatives never
provided enough women for the growth of colonies of Dutch families. Misce­
genation was officially sanctioned by the Company by 1642 and was practised
on all social levels but particularly the most senior. By contrast, unions with
Asian women fonned by the largest component of the European group, the
soldiers and sailors, were often irregular and fleeting, despite efforts by the
VOC's directors to keep in the settlements those men who had married local
women and fathered children there. Children abandoned by the father were
usually absorbed by the Asian community to which the mother belonged.
On the other hand, among the elite there developed a peculiar family pattern
and system of descent. Characteristically, a man delayed marriage (as opposed
to concubinage) until he had risen in the merchant ranks of the civilian hier­
archy. His marriage partner was usually the Asia-born daughter of a colleague,
a girl barely in her teens if it was her first marriage, the product of a secluded
upbringing supervised by Asian slaves, in which styles of dress, personal
171

--- PAGE: 204 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.204) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
hygiene, training, entertainment, and language were all Asian and Asia­
derived. Such women were themselves part-Asian by descent as well as by
upbringing and culture. Their daughters were kept in Asia as brides for new
generations of immigrants, while their sons were sent to the Netherlands to
make their careers and livelihood. Despite years of residence, then, men did not
command patriarchal families nor did the Indies Dutch become landowning
gentry attached to property held within the same family over generations.
Promotion to positions of power and wealth came to men connected to each
other through their wives.
In the VOC centuries many men succumbed to disease, their resistance
lowered by their clinging to clothing and hygiene more suitable for northern
Europe and by their intemperate use of alcohol. Women, living more nearly
according to Asian styles and often being many years junior to their husbands,
tended to survive them. In these conditions there was pressure to remarry,
especially when the widow was the sole inheritor of marital property. Some
women married two and three times, so soon consoling themselves that Ba­
tavia's government adopted, for regulation of European behavior, a Koranic
law observed by Indonesians that forbade remarriage by women before three
months of widowhood had elapsed.
In this, as in many other instances, the Dutch showed themselves profoundly
influenced by the Asian context of their lives. The position of women with
European status is another example, for the semi-purdah of women showed
accommodation to Muslim and Hindu-derived notions of female propriety and
stood against the Calvinist model of virtuous housewife ,and mother in society.
The seigneurial style of living of senior Company officials on country estates,
where the officials were surrounded by slave retainers and took rights to corvee
labor from the local population, owed more to the Javanese nobility than to the
style of the respectable classes of eighteenth-century Netherlands society.
It is interesting to note that before the nineteenth century there was little intel­
lectual exchange between Dutch and Indonesian. Senior representatives of the
Dutch did not impress the Javanese nobility as bearers of a higher civilization,
as they were to do in the late colonial period. The origins and status of the VOC
Dutch were regarded with contempt, and this prevented their mixing, in their
brief encounters with court members, on tenns of equality. During the first two
centuries of contact, then, the Dutch were not able to influence Java's nobility,
nor were they qualified by their own education to admire the culture of the
Indonesian courts.
The greatest contact Dutch people had with Asians was with the subject
populations of their settlements, who were themselves also generally members
of dispossessed social groups. Intellectual exchange between the two was
necessarily limited. Moreover, in considering mutual influence, the intimacy
that existed between European men and Asian women in the settlements must
172

--- PAGE: 205 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.205) ---
EPILOGUE
be balanced against the inequality of their status and rights. The Asian context
was predominant in detennining how the immigrant Dutch lived, and in the
Asian settlements the cultural fonns transmitted by the Dutch were few. De­
spite directives from successive governors-general, the Asian majorities of
Dutch cities never became Dutch speakers or Christians. What was Dutch was
the public architecture of the cities and their layout, and the male life of the
government office.
Mestizo society took on folkways of its own. It was neither European nor
Asian, nor was it static. By the second half of the eighteenth century there
appeared a trend towards development of patriarchal families, and a desire to
cut remaining ties with Europe. As it turned out, the VOC directors were fairly
successful in enforcing the rule that denied senior office to the locally born and
so prevented the development of independent colonies, but the impulse towards
such a development is discernible. The destruction of these ambitions and of
the Mestizo alliance of position and family connection was quick and complete.
It sprang, not out of the history of the Dutch in Indonesia, but from the history
of Netherlands society in Europe, a society that had been transfonned by the
French Revolution and Napoleonic Empire. Earlier pressures on Mestizo soci­
ety--described under the rubrics' 'Enlightenment," "Interregnum," which are
drawn from European history --can best be seen as assaults on Mestizo society
that could not succeed because not aimed at the nexus of post and family
alliance.
New barriers to cultural comprehension, formed in the nineteenth century,
sharpened by a heightened sense of race. The separation was encouraged by the
formation in the European community of families on the Dutch model, as
women emigrated in increasing numbers. Mestizo culture had always been the
medium through which Dutch and Indonesian came to know each other. When
it was under attack, new gulfs opened up, for there was no substitution for the
old immediacy of contact. The very factors which improved the quality of
Dutch life in Indonesia-the links to other communities and to Europe provided
by rail, telegraph, newspaper, library, and the like-all combined to foster
self-sufficient communities among the immigrants who now dominated the top
level of the government hierarchy. These same conditions served to foster new
identities among Indonesian groups that were to be drawn in sharp opposition to
the colonial regime as the twentieth century opened.
Within the European group in the nineteenth century, the elite were divorced
from the mass. The ranks of senior officials ceased to be occupied by men who
spent the greatest part of their lives in Asia and who married Asia-born women.
Now members of the elite were immigrants and transients. Mestizo culture
became more closely associated with lower socioeconomic classes; members
became a distinct subgroup within the European, marrying amongst them­
selves. Absotption into the Indonesian world followed on poverty or a total
173

--- PAGE: 206 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.206) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
separation from the European community and its badge of status, white skin
color.
Nevertheless, the Dutch in Indonesia remained exceedingly mixed. Members
of the new elite boasted of their European identity, yet were not European in the
manner of compatriots who did not emigrate. Their lives were passed in a
tropical setting where they were a privileged minority ruling a majority who
had no say in their governance. In this situation, sexual exploitation and irre­
sponsibility of male immigrants towards Indonesian women servants and their
offspring were condoned. European women, as wives, had to deal with that
indulgence and the social problems that followed. Because they, in their time,
were not yet political members of European society, their views were expressed
first through the colonial novel, before the issues of concubinage and deserted
children were taken up by the male members of the Netherlands' States­
General. In their novels, Dutch women authors revealed an aspect of colonial
life that is nonnally hidden from view.
In the rural areas we see Mestizo society evolving anew in the same condi­
tions of isolation and union of Dutch immigrant men with Indonesian women,
but it was different from the society of the VOC days. For one thing, the
immigrants were now more likely to be planters and private entrepreneurs, with
only a minority representing the Batavian government. For another, a European
reference group was now closer at hand, and there was no avenue for political
life or the need for secure connections, when men could pursue wealth openly
and legally. Assimilation towards the Indonesian side was a greater pull for
Eurasian men, and the marriage partners of Eurasian women were more likely
to be lower-ranking Dutchmen or men from the Eurasian community.
In the twentieth centul)' events overtook Dutch society of the Indies. No
longer the self-contained zone of the VOC centuries, colonial society was split
within and subjected to pressures from without-from the Netherlands and
increasingly from Indonesian groups that sought to take over the seat of power
in Batavia for themselves. Once the ultimate claim to status for the Eurasian
was severed, the tie to the European and the prestige of race, Mestizo society as
a separate entity could not endure. It disappeared in 1942 to live on only in the
sentimental evocations of the Tempo Doeloe writers and recorders.
174

--- PAGE: 207 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.207) ---
New Explorations of
European -Asian Encounters
THE HISTORY NARRATED IN the first edition of The Social World of Batavia
ended with Sukarno's proclamation of Indonesia's independence on 17 Au­
gust 1945. Then the distinctive culture that I had termed Mestizo dissolved
into its constituent parts, and Indonesians and Dutch went their separate
ways. From 1949 the Republic of Indonesia set about distancing the new
state from Holland: English was made the preferred second language in
place of Dutch in school curricula; streets and cities, including Batavia, lost
their Dutch names and acquired Indonesian ones; public symbols of Dutch
rule were erased; new heroes filled the pantheon, and the entrance to every
town acquired a memorial to its fighters for independence. In 1957 Dutch
businesses were seized by the state; in 1959 Dutch people who did not em­
brace Indonesian citizenship were expelled. These were the measures of a
government determined to develop an Indonesian identity and to make
people forget the intertwining of Dutch with Indonesian histories. All the
same, in Indonesia, academic and popular histories, the press, cinema, and
politicians' speeches present the past as a country the Dutch ruled for 350
long years.
In the Netherlands the past also lives on. Explorations of many of the
themes touched on in the earlier edition of The Social World of Batavia
have been published over the last three decades. Celebrations in Holland
in 2002 to mark the four-hundredth anniversary of the VOC's founding
provoked in Indonesia formation of the National Committee for the In­
donesian Nation's Dignity, and official protest to the Dutch by Indonesia's
state minister for national development planning. The opinion page of the
Jakarta Post, the capital's English-language daily, carried an article on 4
May 2002 by historian Asvi Warman Adam of the Indonesian Institute
of Sciences, entitled "Can Indonesia Sue the Netherlands for History?" In
it he called on historians to support demands for compensation by docu­
menting the process whereby the Dutch East Indies Company, or VOC,
began delivering "riches and golden age to the Dutch, but brought poverty,
misery and death to the Indonesians."
175

--- PAGE: 208 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.208) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
Asvi Warman Adam's article raises the question of the relevance of
VOC studies for Indonesians today. VOC studies published since The So­
cial World of Batavia come from Western, not Indonesian, universities and
academic presses. Yet it can be argued that they hold more than antiquar­
ian interest for modern Indonesians. VOC-era surgeons, artists, linguists,
botanists, and mapmakers worked with Asian counterparts. Mostly, we
only know the names of Asian colleagues and co-workers from the nine­
teenth century, but H. A. van Reede tot Drakenstein, principal author of
the multivolume Hortus Malabaricus (published 1678-1679, 1682-1693),
named Ranga Botto, Vinaique Pandito, and Apu Botto, all from Goa, as
his collaborators. The specialist knowledge of many Asians was recorded,
preserved, and disseminated through publication of numerous dictionar­
ies, compendia, natural histories, and illustrated encyclopedias in Holland.
Learned works such as Georg Rumphius's Ambonese Curiosity Cabinet
(published in 1705) and his Ambonese Herbarium (published posthumously
in 1741) and Franc;ois Valentijn's Oud en Nieuw Oost-Indien (1721) went
through several editions and were brought to widening circles of readers
through translations into other languages. European publications preserved
the knowledge of many Asians. Libraries and modern editions give back
that knowledge to their descendants in Indonesia's academies today.
Historical studies in Indonesia are not richly resourced, supported by
research libraries and specialist librarians; scholars have limited opportuni­
ties, either of time or grants, for extensive studies, travel, and publication.
Since the downfall of President Suharto in 1998, Indonesia's historians
have been called on to "straighten" the record of the past (meluruskan seja­
rah).! Many of the academic and popular studies from Indonesian presses
today focus on reinterpreting official heroes and developing regional and
community histories. 2 Interest in the VOC appears limited to a small circle
of specialists, such as the curator of Jakarta's museum, Candrian Atta­
hiyyat, who published VOC: Armada Kapal dan Mitologi (VOC: Fleet and
Mythology) in 1991 and has written numerous articles on the preservation
of "Kota," the "old town" of today's megapolis of Jakarta. 3
Translations by Indonesians of historical studies by contemporary
Western academics show there is renewed interest in Indonesia in foreign
scholarship on the nation's past. 4 And in Holland, too, decades after the
severing of Indonesia from the Netherlands, there is recognition that Dutch
history includes narratives of Indonesia and Indonesians. Dutch govern­
ment ministries now finance research that documents the experiences and
actions of Dutch people in the East and a spate of books aimed at Dutch
citizens whose family roots lie in Indonesia. 5
In 1986 Gerrit Knaap published a detailed study of the VOC in Ambon,
the end point of a historic trade route, under the title Kruidnagelen en Chris-
176

--- PAGE: 209 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.209) ---
NEW EXPLORATIONS OF EUROPEAN-ASIAN ENCOUNTERS
tenen (Cloves and Christians).6 In it he traced demographic impacts on the
Ambonese from their encounters with the new global player. Contributors
to Ships, Sailors and Spices examined the history of Dutch shipping and the
personnel of the floating worlds of the East Indiamen, as the ships built for
Asia voyages were called.7 Femme Gaastra's 1982 Geschiedenis van de VOC,
published in English in 2003 as The Dutch East India Company, covers the
founding and operations of the VOC, with details on directors, financing,
and early expeditions to Indonesian islands, and on trading settlements
in India and mainland Southeast Asia. He discusses connections between
these far-flung outposts, gives biographical sketches of VOC "characters,"
and traces the rise and decline of the Company. 8 Harm Stevens's study,
Dutch Enterprise and the VOC, focused on the worldwide commerce con­
ducted by the VOC.9
Leonard Blusse's studies enlarged understanding of VOC-era Batavia
through his special attention to the city's Japanese and Chinese commu­
nities. In a 1981 article he had described Holland's Asian headquarters
as a Chinese colonial town because it was a manufacturing and commer­
cial hub, within Chinese shipping and trading networks in Southeast Asia,
producing almost exclusively for the China market. 10 (Carl Trocki sub­
sequently described Southeast Asia as China's zone of offshore produc­
tion for mining, agriculture, and financial services.)11Susan Abeyasekere's
Jakarta: A History brought the narrative of Indonesia's premier city up
to 1985.12
In 1923, Batavia's archivist, F. de Haan, had brought out a volume of
plates to accompany his Oud Batavia Gedenkboek, published the previous
year. In addition to drawings and paintings of places, he included pho­
tographs of old canvases showing Batavia street scenes and VOC "per­
sonalities." Both volumes were reprinted in 1935. In 1982 Adolf Heuken
published an illustrated Historical Sites of Jakarta (what was left of them
in Jakarta's rapid redevelopment), complementing De Haan's old photo­
graphs with colored contemporary shots.13 An update on De Haan's work
can be seen in Batavia in Nineteenth Century Photographs published by
Scott Merrillees in 2000.14 His emphasis was on Batavia's growth between
the 1860s and 1890s and how the built environment shaped and reflected
the city's residents. In this book Merrillees gives the location and a brief
history of each building with notes on its demolition or renovations and
function in today's Jakarta. In 1985, the Rijksmuseum acquired 578 works
by Batavia's premier illustrator, Jan Brandes; 198 of them are reproduced
in color in The World of Jan Brandes, 1743-1808, edited by Max de Bruijn
and Remco Raben. 15 Here are Batavians, Batavian residences, including
Brandes's own villa, street and market scenes, and local flora and fauna,
along with chapters on Brandes's life and art.
177

--- PAGE: 210 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.210) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
Brandes left portraits of himself and, it is believed, of Roosje, an Indo­
nesian domestic in his employ, with whom he formed a long-term sexual
relationship. In 2006 I published meditations on VOC society flowing from
a consideration of the Cnoll family portrait, painted in 1665 by Jacob
Jansz. Coeman,I6 and the following year I made a study of a handful of
seventeenth-century portraits that survive of women whose livestook them
from Holland to Cape Town, Melaka, and Batavia.17 All these women were
elevated far above Roosje's status, secure as wives and daughters of Euro­
pean men. They are painted in outdoors settings, framed by curtains and
balustrades. A background of sea and shipping detaches them from their
immediate context. With the exception of the Cnoll portrait, no object
links the painted ladies to place; only their dress locates them in time.
In the first edition of Social World I made the case that VOC society
became detached from Holland and developed a distinct identity that was
neither Dutch nor Asian, but Mestizo, and I argued that this society had
fully evolved by the middle years of the eighteenth century. No portraits
of VOC ladies apparently survive from that century. Realistic portraiture
was not characteristic of the Indonesian societies that bordered Batavia.
Javanese ladies were known by titles derived from father or husband. They
were not exposed to public gaze either in person or via the counterfeit form
of the portrait. Considering the portraits we do have from the seventeenth
century, noting the attention their artists paid to facial features to reveal in­
dividual identity and personality, so typical of portrait painting in Holland
of the time, I now see in these early VOC portraits evidence of the sitters'
inclination to remain Dutch and claim Dutch status.
Thirteen letters, written by Johanna Maria van Riebeeck in 1710 to her
parents in Batavia from on board ship bound for Holland and from the
long stopover at the Cape, also suggest a reconsideration of my construc­
tion of Mestizo society.I8Johanna was second-generation VOC, having
been born and raised in Batavia. Her mother, Elisabeth van Oosten, from
early childhood was also a Batavian. Johanna's father had been born in the
VOC settlement at the Cape of Good Hope. After a spell in Ceylon where
her first husband was governor, Johanna returned to Batavia. She spent
three years as first lady of Dutch Asia following her marriage in 1706 to
Governor-General Joan van Hoorn. She was not one of those VOC ladies
who lacked command of Dutch. She not only spoke it, but she was also
capable of composing in it. From her pen there are: reports on the state of
health of passengers on the return fleet; lists of goods taken aboard and
acquired at the Cape to be dispatched to Batavia relatives; description of
the VOC's Cape settlement and of a trip to the governor's country estate;
impressions of VOC employees at the Cape and recommendations for ca­
reer moves to her father (then the governor-general in Batavia). All this is
178

--- PAGE: 211 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.211) ---
NEW EXPLORATIONS OF EUROPEAN-ASIAN ENCOUNTERS
interspersed with formulaic hopes for God's blessing and anticipation of
the "dear patria."
Patria was not Java. The homeland so reverently invoked was the Hol­
land Johanna van Riebeeck had never seen. She was only traveling to it
because her husband was under orders from the Company directors to sail
with the return fleet on stepping down from the highest VOC office. The
couple's request for retirement in Batavia had been denied. Johanna's let­
ters suggest consciousness of rank and assimilation of a Netherlands iden­
tity that transcended the local. In her we find the Creole lady who insists on
a European identity to preserve status wherever her marriage career might
take her in Asia, Africa, or Holland. The publication of her letters by Bos­
man in 1952 was part of a project of celebrating "Riebeeckiana."
Bosman's volume also contains family records drawn up by Jan van Rie­
beeck, the Cape's founder, notes of a return visit there by Abraham van
Riebeeck in 1676, and three of his letters to his daughter, plus Johanna's
provisions book. That context gives to the letter reader of today a sense of
Johanna's being a part of Netherlands history more than of VOC Asian
societies. We hear her voice when she has left Batavia forever and is prepar­
ing for a new beginning in Europe.
In 1991 John Landwehr and Peter van der Krogt published a compre­
hensive bibliography of all printed works produced by the VOC in the
Netherlands and by VOC-licensed printers in Batavia and Ceylon. 19 The
materials are grouped into three categories: single-page items such as plac­
ards and poems; pamphlet-length publications of sailing instructions, sal­
ary lists, collections of prayers and the like; and books. The compilers give
the full title of each entry, provide brief notes on contents, and identify the
archive where the original is held. Indexes of titles and authors offer entry
to this massive bibliography. It yields the unexpected detail. For example,
the painter of the Cnoll family emerges as subject of a poem penned by
Jacob Steendam and printed on satin in honor of Coeman's marriage to
Juffr. Judith Augustynus in Batavia in 1671 (entry 1553).
Landwehr's bibliography of printed books establishes the VOC as a ma­
jor sponsor of scholarship in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries. The
Company financed printing of histories of new regions in which its em­
ployees now worked, such as Japan. It underwrote the cost of natural his­
tory investigations. It preserved Asian knowledge of marine life and botany
through publication of expensive volumes, lavishly illustrated, and through
the setting up of curiosity cabinets. The VOC's role as producer of scientific
information is examined by the contributors to Bethlehem's and Meijer's
VOC en cultuur. 20 It includes biographical studies of early Japan scholars
Engelbert Kaempfer and Isaac Titsingh and chapters on the VOC's impor­
tant contribution to knowledge of Asian societies by financing translations
179

--- PAGE: 212 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.212) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
from Asian languages into Dutch and publication of many dictionaries. In
the Bethlehem and Meijer collection Jurrien van Goor examines the close
connection between trade and science, and this theme is explored at great
length in Harold 1. Cook's Matters of Exchange. 21 The VOC and the physi­
cal and social sciences are the subject of a recent collection of essays by
Leonard Blusse and Ilonka Ooms, Kennis en Compagnie.22
It is also important to establish that the VOC put printed books into In­
donesian hands from early in the seventeenth century. For example, 10,000
copies of the Reformed Church's catechism were printed in Ambonese in
1623. This multiplication of books occurred at a time in Islamic history
when bans on printing in Arabic language and script, issued and upheld by
successive Ottoman caliphs until the nineteenth century, restricted books
for a Muslim readership to handwritten form and therefore to very limited
numbers. VOC publications also shed light on the progress of Islamizing
in the archipelago. For example, Batavia's press gives a picture of Islamic
practice in the archipelago in the middle of the eighteenth century in its
compendium of Muslim customary laws then being observed or recalled,
including those concerning marriage and inheritance. The numbers of
printed books in Malay were sufficient to warrant a bibliography by 1736.
In that year, Landwehr's compilation tells us, Batavia's VOC press pub­
lished a bibliography of Malay books by both European and Indigenous
authors and also a list of unpublished manuscripts in Gothic, Roman, Ara­
bic, and Malay characters (entry 759).
The VOC Bibliography contains 1,674 entries. Inevitably, the well-to-do
and the litigious found their way into print. They figure in numerous plac­
ards, regulations, instructions, and poems of circumstance. Book-length
biographical studies of VOC personnel and their Asian colleagues remain
scarce. There are short studies of Dutch, Chinese, and Indian merchants in
All of One Company: The VOC in Biographical Perspective. One of them,
the merchant Jan Albert Sichterman, developed a love for Chinese porce­
lain, Japanese lacquer ware, and Indian ivory. His biographer, Christiaan
1. A. Jorg, tells us that Sichterman's possessions filled two ships of the return
fleet to Holland in 1745 and were displayed in a grand house built to his own
design in Groningen. 23 The subtitle of Karel Schoeman's Afrikaans-language
Kinders van die Kompanjie is "Cape Life in the Seventeenth CenturY,"24but
many of the people he selected for mini-biographies traveled widely within
VOC Asia. There are entries, for instance, for the scientists and administra­
tors Rumphius, Willem ten Rhijne, and Rijcklof van Goens, but also for
"Sijmon die Arabier, tolk" (The Arab Simon, interpreter) and "Angela van
Bengale, vryswartin" (Angela from Bengal, free Black woman).
Eyewitness accounts of a range of VOC employees are assembled in
Roeper and Van Gelder's In dienst van de Compagnie. 25 Alfons van der
180

--- PAGE: 213 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.213) ---
NEW EXPLORATIONS OF EUROPEAN-ASIAN ENCOUNTERS
Kraan has published studies of Rijcklof van Goens and Jeremias van
Vliet.26 Leonard Blusse developed a chapter from his 1986 collection of
essays27into a full-length study of Cornelia van Nieuwenroode, the central
lady in the Cnoll painting by Coeman. 28 Asian royals are the subject of
biographical chapters in Hoi en handel (Court and Commerce). Mention
can be made of Leonard Andaya's study of Sultanah Safiatuddin Shah,
ruler of Aceh between 1641 and 1675.29 Aceh's succession of female rulers
is the subject of a doctoral thesis from the University of London, "Rule
behind the Silk Curtain: A Study of the Sultanahs of Aceh 1641-1699" by
Sher Banu Khan.
Other scholars have studied individuals who negotiated multiple worlds,
such as female traders, free burgers, and the religious. Dhiravat na Pombe­
jra took as subject Osoet Pegua, a Mon woman whose business life as a
broker between the VOC and the Siamese court is established in Company
records, as well as details of her domestic life that included bearing children
to two Dutch men stationed in Ayutthaya in the first decades of the seven­
teenth century.30In 2003, William Bradley Horton revived interest in the
part-Siamese, part-German Pieter Erberveld whom the VOC had convicted
of conspiring with the Javanese Raden Kartadria to kill all Christians in
Batavia and had subsequently executed in 1722.31 Horton's article reviewed
Dutch scholarship on the facts and fiction of the Erberveld story, but his
major focus was on Malay and later Indonesian language versions of it.
He traced accounts from an 1889 Chinese-Malay newspaper (reproduced
in the article in full) through to an Indonesian television drama version
from the 1960s. Mayumi Yamamoto complemented this study by investi­
gating Japanese-language sources relating to Erberveld. Her focus is on the
monument erected by the Dutch as a deterrent to other would-be rebels.
She explains that the Japanese military, during their occupation of Java, re­
interpreted Erberveld as a "half-caste" independence fighter.32 Erberveld's
head and a plaque detailing his crimes had remained a fixture of colonial
Batavia. De Haan's Platenalbum shows the Erberveld monument as it was
in colonial times; Heuken's photograph is of the same monument following
its reinstallation in the 1980s.
Other scholars have mined VOC handwritten documents in Holland and
overseas archives. For example, Ruurdje Laarhoven went through volumes
of receipts and bills of lading to study the trade in textiles between In­
dia and Java. Her study demonstrated the importance of the Java market
and how the VOC sought to identify and satisfy the tastes of Javanese
consumers. 33
Notary records provide source material for important new studies. Hen­
drik Niemeijer has scrutinized manumission records from the wills made by
Asian and European owners in a study of Batavia's free(d) Asian Christian
181

--- PAGE: 214 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.214) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
community. He has also established that VOC institutions provided mecha­
nisms for Indigenous peoples to air and possibly resolve disputes over land,
house title, and other property. 34 Remco Raben has also investigated the
communities that grew up around Batavia's walls.35 He argues that mobility
was key to the emergence of the Betawi as a distinct community, and he
dates its formation to the eighteenth century. Old Dutch documents de­
scribe Batavia's environs as a series of villages, each inhabited by a separate
ethnic group under its own headman. In reality, Raben finds villages had
constantly changing, multi-ethnic populations. Men left for stints in Dutch
armies. Emancipated slaves and newcomers moved in. Individuals crossed
ethnic and residential boundaries through marriage, assumed different
identities, fused into Betawi or Batavians. Not so, argue representatives of
this group today. They descend from princes of Sunda Kelapa, Banten, and
Demak who introduced Islam to the region.36 Most importantly, they claim
Indigenous rights to valuable real estate in today's Jakarta.
Eric Jones turned to another source, the records of local law courts to
which Asian residents of Batavia and environs brought their slaves to de­
mand punishment for behaviors ranging from rudeness, assault, and theft
to flight. Clerks took down sworn statements by owners and also slaves;
they recorded testimony from the accuseds' friends and accomplices who
were themselves slave personnel from an array of Batavia's businesses and
households. The court's clerks translated their testimony into Dutch and
recorded the aldermen's judicial rulings. Here, in the trials Jones recon­
structs of Danie, Poedak, Christina van Ambon, Sitie van Makasar, and
Esperanca van Bugis, can be heard the voices of slaves, long ago punished
and dead, complaining of ill treatment, describing their feelings of hurt,
offense, or rage, justifying their actions against perceived injustices. Oc­
casionally there is a hint of a Dutchman's personal views on slavery in the
magistrates' rulings as they comment on the alleged crimes and give expla­
nations for the thinking behind the sentences they issued.37
In the seventeenth century the Dutch were more on sea than on land.
Port settlements were turned outward: the sea was their lifeline. When the
rulers of Banten and Mataram sent armies to attack the fledgling Dutch
settlement, it was ships and sea lanes that saved the tiny republic, and it was
ships that connected each settlement in the Indonesian archipelago to the
others set down across southern Africa, Iraq, India, Indochina, Malaya,
and Japan between the 1630s and 1660s. VOC ships carried the Company
personnel and their female kin who circulated between settlements in the
course of office and marriage careers. Historians of the Indian Ocean have
popularized the concept of littoral societies. Remco Raben has made a
comparative study of the inhabitants of Batavia and Colombo. 38 New stud­
ies await scholars who would bring all the VOC societies together.
182

--- PAGE: 215 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.215) ---
NEW EXPLORATIONS OF EUROPEAN-ASIAN ENCOUNTERS
One of the most innovative research projects of the last decade was
TANAP (Towards a New Age of Partnership), launched by the Na­
tional Archives of the Netherlands and the Research School for Asian,
African, and Amerindian Studies of Leiden University. The overall aim
was to further the development of early modern world history by mak­
ing accessible the 25 million pages of VOC documents generated by VOC
officials and the Asian kings, nobles, merchants, harbor masters, and
seamen with whom they interacted. Goals included the preservation or
recovery and study of VOC documents in depositories around Asia and
Africa, scholarships for training a new generation of scholars, and tran­
scription of documents important for local histories. In this project, young
historians from twelve universities in Asia participated in a master's de­
gree that included training in deciphering seventeenth-century Dutch
handwriting.
TANAP graduates subsequently embarked on academic theses based on
VOC documents in the national libraries and archives of their home coun­
tries. The first published results expand our knowledge of Asian-European
interactions in the early modern period. Anjana Singh's social history of
the VOC settlement at Fort Cochin and its surroundings on the Malabar
Coast develops local and regional histories and traces the emergence of
new social groups such as Christian Mestizas.39 She shows how Indigenous
people and traveling Asian merchants made use of VOC legal institutions
to resolve commercial and personal disputes. Hoang Anh Tuan's Silk for
Silver focuses on trade, political, and cultural relations between Vietnam­
ese of the Trinh-ruled state and the VOC in the years 1637 to 1700.40 In it
he documents how the VOC stimulated local silk and ceramic industries
and integrated Tonkin merchants and goods into the intra-Asian trade. He
identifies VOC goals and Vietnamese ambitions: the Dutch wanted trade
goods they could not easily obtain from China; the Trinh rulers wanted
Dutch firearms, ammunition, and military expertise to further campaigns
against the rival Nguyen southern state.
In Cape Town a group of transcribers for TEPC (Transcription of Es­
tate Papers of the Cape) have been making available online inventories of
wills and auctions made there between 1652 and 1795 by VOC officials,
European settlers, freed Africans, and also Indonesians and Chinese. These
last two communities stemmed from exiles and convicts sent by Batavia to
the Cape. There, in the lists of spoons and teacups, pictures, books, chairs,
clothes, and other items of material culture, is a wealth of information that
opens up possibilities for discovering histories of the ancestors of today's
South Africans. Like Jones's work with slave testimonies, this transcrip­
tion project brings to the fore social groups hitherto part of the "grey and
undifferentiated" mass.

--- PAGE: 216 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.216) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
Two recent studies of the Cape settlement are M. A. M. Verstegen's
De Indische Zeeherberg (The Indies Tavern by the Sea)41 and Dan Sleigh's
Afrikaans-language Die buiteposte (The Outer Settlements).42 Nigel
Worden assesses recent scholarship in his "New Approaches to VOC His­
tory in South Africa."43Fresh work on VOC settlements includes a study of
Galle by Lodewijk Wagenaar. 44 "Biographies" of settlements are comple­
mented by studies of Asian kingdoms and capitals. Dhiravat na Pombejra
mined VOC sources for studies of Ayutthaya and the Dutch trading center
there.45 In 1656 the VOC's Rijcklof van Goens had written a report for
the Company's directors on his five postings to Java's Mataram kingdom
between 1648 and 1654. It was first released to the general reading public
in 1667 as Javaense Reyse (Java Journey) and republished in 1995 with an
introduction and annotations by Darja de Wever. In it Van Goens gives
detailed descriptions of the Javanese countryside, Mataram's administra­
tive apparatus, and the complicated and dangerous life of courtiers serving
Amangkurat 11.46
Luc Nagtegaal drew on VOC eyewitness accounts to detail the workings
of Javanese royalty in his doctoral thesis that was published as Riding the
Dutch Tiger in 1996.47 Merle Ricklefs's detailed studies of Java, for example,
his The Seen and Unseen Worlds in Java, are based on a close comparison
of Javanese and VOC records. 48 Peter Carey and Vincent Houben used
Javanese chronicles, letters, and genealogies to investigate the contribution
of women to Javanese palace life.49 They concluded that women connected
to the royal families and the nobility had greater freedom and input into
political life at the beginning of the nineteenth century in comparison with
protocols governing elite women at that century's end. Ann Kumar, in an
extensive study, presented court life through the special female perspective
of a royal guard. 50 Her comparison of Javanese and VOC chronicles estab­
lished that their authors had very similar concerns and judgments on such
contemporary issues as declining prosperity, increasing social violence, and
outbreaks of smallpox. 51 Overall, she concludes that while in Europe the
VOC was a force for modernization, in Java the consequences of its modern
methods of accounting, reporting, and organization were to tie Javanese
more firmly to the land, entrench despotic rule, and suppress inquiry.
Chapters in Jakarta-Batavia, such as "JABOTABEK Place Names" and
"Jakarta Greenery" draw on VOC and later Dutch records to link past to
present. 52 Van Gelder and Wagenaar identify the VOC's lingering impact
in the Netherlands on Dutch material culture and society through chapters
on old warehouses, shipyards, food, and drink in Sporen van de Compagnie
(Traces of the Company).53 The late Professor M. A. P. Meilink-Roelofsz
and her colleagues highlighted the impact of Dutch scholarship on Asian
historiography in an anthology of English translations of excerpts from
184

--- PAGE: 217 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.217) ---
NEW EXPLORATIONS OF EUROPEAN-ASIAN ENCOUNTERS
work written between 1865 and 1976. An example is De Haan's "Everyday
Life in Batavia."54
Commemoration in Holland of the four-hundredth anniversary of the
VOC's founding resulted in a spate of books, many of them lavishly il­
lustrated, pitched at readerships scholarly and popular. Mention can be
made of the Vademecum van de Oost- en West-Indische Compagnie of Peter
van Wiechen,55 The Colourful World of the VOC by Leo Akveld and Els
Jacobs,56Hollanders uit en thuis (Hollanders Abroad and at Home),57 and
two major volumes from Kees Zandvliet. The Rijksmuseum's historian and
archivist with special interest in the Indonesia collections published Map­
ping for Money in this quatercentenary year.58 It traces the expansion of
European knowledge of Asian and American coastlines, discusses char­
acteristic perspectives of maps, gives vignettes of important figures in the
history of cartography, as well as discussing the VOC's substantial contri­
butions to mapmaking. He also assembled, in The Dutch Encounter with
Asia, an extraordinary array of paintings, portraits, and objects made by
Dutch artists between 1600 and 1950.59 Some of the inclusions, such as
marine scenes and Andries Beeckman's 1657 painting of Batavia's market
and castle, are well known, but Zandvliet has brought from obscurity such
important works as the portrait by an unknown Dutch artist of Sultan
Saifudin of Tidore (r. 1657-1707). His is one of the few Indonesian faces
known to us from the seventeenth century.
In 1999, Marie-Odette Scalliet and her fellow contributors produced a
treasure trove of artwork in Pictures from the Tropics.60 The scope of the
term "Dutch Colonial Period" in the subtitle covers VOC works as well as
those created in the Netherlands Indies era. Haks and Maris had performed
an important service for historians seeking documentation on the foreign
artists who worked in the Indonesian archipelago through publication in
1995 of a comprehensive listing of them together with mini-biographies. 61
Fascination with pictures from the past and recognition of their value
for history writing continue in a number of recent works. Annabel Teh
Gallop edited an important collection of early nineteenth-century illustra­
tions made by artists and engravers working in the Raffles era of Java's
history.62The accompanying text is in both Indonesian and English. There
are drawings of Javanese laborers stripping back vines and creepers from
the Borobudur, sketches of pony transports and ferry crossings, paintings
of Javanese plowing and planting rice fields, relaxing around food stalls,
or intent on a cock fight. In one remarkable watercolor by John Newman,
two men, a European and a Javanese, sit stiffly on chairs opposite each
other, wine glasses raised as in a toast, retainers on hand, and a framed
painting of European sailing ships on the wall. The painting suggests the
impact on this artist of the Javanese two-dimensional style of illustration.
185

--- PAGE: 218 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.218) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
Gallop's album is also significant because it includes botanical studies that
are the work of anonymous Indian and Chinese artists working in Euro­
pean styles and paintings by unknown Javanese artists. Some of the latter
are examples of Java's illuminated manuscript tradition. In addition there
is a detailed representation, dating from the early nineteenth century, of
a royal procession painted in wayang-style, but showing the influence of
European three-dimensional techniques.
VOC-era materials continue to support new fields of research in South­
east Asian Studies. An example is the work now starting on sensory his­
tory. Alain Corbin, Roy Porter, and other historians of Europe have shown
how sensory evidence can be extracted from histories of technology, mu­
nicipal regulations, diaries and novels, and landscape design. Raquel Reyes
and Peter Boomgaard are pioneering Southeast Asian sensory histories
through studies of urban smells and sound. Johannes Rach's drawings
of eighteenth-century Batavia's streets, rutted by carriage-wheels, and of
canals that were receptacles for human waste and urban detritus provide
material for the resourceful researcher. 63
I have focused on sampling studies using VOC sources in this review
of the past quarter century of research because it is the VOC chapters of
the first edition of Social World that made the greatest impact. I brought
my history of Mestizo society to an end through its slow unmaking in the
nineteenth century. For insights into that century I drew on newspaper ad­
vertisements in the "personals" columns, on colonial literature in the broad
sense of travelers' accounts, pamphlets, magazines, stories, and novels, and
on photographs in Nieuwenhuys's 1961 collection and in the KITLV Ar­
chive. Nieuwenhuys subsequently brought out three more photograph al­
bums to form a Tempo Doeloe series: Baren en oudgasten (Newcomers and
Old-Timers) in 1981, Komen en blijven (Coming and Settling) in 1982,
and Met vreemde oogen (Through Strange Eyes) in 1998.64 Wim Willems and
his colleagues followed in the tradition of Nieuwenhuys by enriching their
1997 book of Indies family histories with drawings, paintings, and photo­
graphs from VOC and later eras.65
The work of important photographers, such as Woodbury and Page,
and Jean Demmeni, was showcased by Steven Wachlin66 and Leo Haks 67 in
the 1980s and 1990s. In 1991 Gerrit Knaap introduced Indonesia's first In­
digenous professional photographer, Kassian Cephas, through a biography
richly illustrated with his works.68 Cephas was a bridge between worlds: the
Javanese who mastered the colonial's technology, who became a Christian,
who won commissions from Yogyakarta's royals to immortalize them in
photographs, who left a visual record of Java's antiquities as well as of its
modern world of steam locomotive and gas light. His application to be-
186

--- PAGE: 219 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.219) ---
NEW EXPLORATIONS OF EUROPEAN-ASIAN ENCOUNTERS
come "equalized" -that is, made European in legal rights and status-was
granted by the colonial government in 1891.
Peter Boomgaard has tapped into the huge photographic collection of
the Tropenmuseum with his 2001 Het Indie Boek. 69 Gajah Mada Univer­
sity's Bambang Purwanto, a specialist in nineteenth-century economic his­
tory and peasant studies, has also turned to the Dutch photographic record
to develop histories of daily life of the past century.70
Some descendants of Indies clans, with branches in the Netherlands and
in Indonesia, have published their own family's albums. A recent example is
Het album van Mientje, a collection of 105 photographs assembled on the
occasion of her marriage in Batavia in 1862.71 Mientje was Wilhelmina van
der Hucht, member of a group of Indies families (Van der Hucht, Holle,
Van den Berg, and Kerkhoven) who were closely connected by marriage
and by business interests in tea plantations in West Java. They form the
subject of Hella Haase's Heren van de Thee (Lords of Tea).72Their diaries,
letters, and memoirs also furnished source material for her collection of
short stories on Indies life that was published in English translation as
Forever a Stranger.73
The late Pramoedya Ananta Toer collected Malay-language fiction writ­
ten by Javanese, Chinese, and Eurasians that revealed interactions and mu­
tual influences between these communities from the reverse perspective. In
1982 the Jakarta publishing house Hasta Mitra published an anthology of
these stories and rhymed fiction with a historical introduction. Pramoedya
called this collection, salvaged from his personal library, Tempo Doeloe.74
He also demonstrated his wide and deep knowledge of historical mate­
rials in the quartet of novels tracing the birth of Indonesia: Bumi Manu­
sia (1980), Anak Semua Bangsa (1981), Jejak Langkah (1985), and Rumah
Kaca (1988),75and in his lengthy novel of Majapahit, Arus Balik, to which
he gave the subtitle "An Epic of the Maritime World."76
There have been numerous studies of colonial fiction by scholars such as
E. M. Beekman, Tessel Pollmann, Elsbeth Locher-Scholten, Tineke Hell­
wig, Charles Coppel, and Christine Pitt. 77 In particular, the figure of the
nyai has been explored. Nyai stories were also standard fare in the popular
Komedie Stamboel. Touring theater companies with Eurasian actors and
Chinese finance performed plays from a varied repertoire in the Malay lan­
guage to audiences composed of a broad ethnic and social band in Java's
towns large and small. The troupe founded in Surabaya in 1891, of which
Auguste Mahieu was the most important of its actor-managers, first per­
formed the famous Nyai Dasima in 1900.78 In versions of the nyai story by
Indies and Indonesian authors and playwrights the heroine was variously
a tragically wronged woman, a romantic figure, a shrewd operator, or a
187

--- PAGE: 220 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.220) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
symbol of colonial exploitation. The 1970 Indonesian movie Samiun dan
Dasima covered all these themes and also portrayed the nyai as longing for
reunion with her own people and the freedom to perform her Islamic obli­
gations openly.79In a 1996 article I compared versions of the Nyai Dasima
story published between 1896 and 1965 in Dutch and variants of Batavia
Malay with the film version of Nyai Dasima and discussed changes to the
story in relation to the period of their production. 80
For the historical anthropologist Ann Laura Stoler, the nyai was a start­
ing point for exploring colonial conditions of race and gender. In research­
ing Capitalism and Confrontation in Sumatra's Plantation Belt Stoler docu­
mented that most incidents of violence among the plantation work force
were not cases of exploited coolie striking back at capitalist manager, but
were in fact between the laborers themselves.81 Chinese and Javanese men
fought each other over sexual access to the few women workers among
them. In a transposition of "nyai studies" to Sumatra, Stoler found that
women's salaries were lower than the men's because employers judged
them, as many Indonesians did, to belong to society's dregs, women who
would casually and entrepreneurially supplement their wages through sell­
ing themselves to fellow coolies after the day's work in fields or factory was
done. Stoler has explored race and gender relations in colonial contexts of
the Netherlands Indies and Vietnam in numerous publications, and she has
developed themes of race, gender, and power in major studies such as her
2002 Carnal Knowledge and Imperial Power.82 The translation into English
and reprinting in 1979 and 1982 of novels from the 1930s by the Szekely
pair, with introductions by historian Anthony Reid, also contributed to the
interest in social relations within tropical colonies.83
Pramoedya Ananta Toer made a nyai the dominant character of the
story-within-the-story in Bumi Manusia. In the colonial-era novels of
P. A. Daum, the nyai had figured as teacher: through her the European
man learned Malay language and norms and how to go about managing his
domestic staff and workforce. Nyai as white man's teacher pervades all the
tangled plots of love, lust, superstition, revenge attacks, and suicide. Most
commentators on Pramoedya's fiction have dwelled on the Ontosoroh story
as exemplifying the evil of colonial racism. But in Pramoedya's historically
grounded treatment of the Indonesian woman/European man relation­
ship, it is important to note that he shows the white man as the teacher
who guides the nyai into European culture and skills. Nyai Ontosoroh has
learned Dutch from him, so that she speaks, reads, and writes with fluency
in the language of the colony's rulers. She has learned how to manage his
dairy business-how to market its products, keep the accounts, and run a
profitable enterprise. She has also learned how to shoulder the burden of
career and child raising alone, for her partner is sunk into alcohol-induced
188

--- PAGE: 221 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.221) ---
NEW EXPLORATIONS OF EUROPEAN-ASIAN ENCOUNTERS
stupor by the time the story begins, and it is Nyai Ontosoroh who steers the
course through Indies society for their Eurasian children.
The photographic record that Nieuwenhuys first popularized similarly
demonstrates the dynamism of interaction, rather than the passivity of
the Native in colonial history. As one example we may consider the sewing
machine. The improved version by I. M. Singer was first manufactured in
1850. After 1870, when migration to the Indies by Dutch women took off,
the sewing machine arrived in Java. The immigrant mistress quickly turned
it over to her Javanese seamstress, who learned how to operate the sewing
machine and supply the household's needs. Here, in the humble images of
the seamstress seated on the floor before a Singer, is evidence of a rapid
transfer of technology and a willingness to acquire new skills. Singer's
machine was operated by hand or foot pedal; it was portable. Javanese
women put this latest technology to use in Indigenous homes and home­
based businesses. They transferred the technology to Indigenous men who
staffed the tailors' shops that sprang up in colonial towns across the archi­
pelago as European and Asian customers alike demanded ready-to-wear
work clothes and uniforms.
Sewing technology and the textile and garment industry have proved
productive sites for exploring relationships between the various communi­
ties of the Netherlands East Indies. Inger McCabe Elliot, Robyn Maxwell,
Alit Veldhuisen-Djajasoebrata, M. 1. de Raadt-Apell, and the contributors
to volume 8 of Indonesia Indah are some of the specialists who have ex­
plored developments in the batik industry in terms of mass production,
improved cottons and dyes, and new designs and designers.84 Batik studies
naturally led to studies of who wore batik, how, and in which settings. In
the first edition of Social World I focused on the kain kebaya, on changes to
style of blouse and batik patterns of the wrap, and on the women who wore
this costume. It seemed a distinctive characteristic of Dutch colonialism
that European women adopted and popularized variants of Indigenous
costume for wear at home and in commissioned photographs. By contrast,
English women in colonial-era India are not generally associated with
wearing the sari or shalwar kameez. I traced a history of change as kain
kebaya became relegated to house wear for women with European status
by the late nineteenth century. Simultaneously it became "traditional" dress
for Javanese women because cheap batik cloth was then coming within
their purchasing power. The technique of applying patterns on to cloth by
metal stamps replaced hand painting of designs. Production through the
putting-out system and factory quickly brought batik lengths to shops and
market stalls across Java.
Following Indonesia's independence, the kain kebaya was propelled to
the national stage by President Sukarno and later by Siti Hartinah Suharto
189

"Transfer of Technology," 1913. Operation of the sewing machine passed from European mistress of the
house to her servants, and through them the technology spread to village households and to tailor shops
in the new colonial towns. (KITLV 32970)
