--- PAGE: 223 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.223) ---
NEW EXPLORATIONS OF EUROPEAN-ASIAN ENCOUNTERS
when she was first lady of Indonesia. In a 1996 article I examined how the
Western suit for men also changed meaning in the colony.85In the Nether­
lands it was the dress of the middle-class man who had won the vote, the
preferred clothing of the Dutch democrat-in-making; but in the colony it
was the dress of Dutch men in civil service, armed forces, and commercial
enterprises who exercised near autocratic powers over Indonesians. It be­
came the desired dress of Indonesian men who aspired to replace them. In
photographs of the historic moment of the declaration of independence,
Sukarno and his male leadership group wear the Western suit, the single
female politician among them and the principal women onlookers wear
kain kebaya. At the moment of the nation's birth, men seem to be grasp­
ing the future as a modern-day governing class, whereas women seem to
represent the nation's essence, a particular and local past rather than the
contemporary and global.
In later publications, dress was interpreted more broadly to include ac­
cessories and body adornment, and researchers further explored histories
of modernization and modernity. In Outward Appearances: Dressing State
and Society in Indonesia, uniforms, shoes, trousers, and head cover drew
attention to ethnic and gender relations; to changing patterns of religious
practice; to the ability of the individual, through dress, to demonstrate mul­
tiple affiliations; and to dress as badge of an individual's class and political
affinities.86
In the years of New Order government, kain kebaya was the habitual
dress worn by Mrs. Suharto in Indonesia and on state visits abroad, and it
became the uniform of women in their state-defined "functions" as wife,
manager of the household, mother, socializer of children, and citizen. Fol­
lowing President Suharto's fall from power in 1998 the kain kebaya seemed
a tainted symbol of KKN, the trio of letters that in Indonesian stands for
collusion, corruption, and nepotism. Now the kain kebaya has traveled to
the pages of Indonesia's glossy fashion magazines and wealthy women's
wardrobes, reinterpreted by a new generation of fashion designers for the
young individualist's nightlife and conspicuous display.87
Raden Ajeng Kartini and her sisters habitually wore kain kebaya and
saw it as demonstrating that they remained authentically Javanese even as
they adopted for their own Western ideals of individual liberty and devel­
oping personal potential. Kartini studies flourished in the 1980s. The late
Syumanjaya directed the film R. A. Kartini, which was released in 1983.88
This film forms an interesting counterpart to the letters and Kartini studies
for it downplays the relationships Kartini conducted with Dutch people
through correspondence in favor of the more visually dramatic relation­
ships conducted directly with members of her family circle. The demand
of the film medium for action sets Kartini firmly in a Javanese context of
191

--- PAGE: 224 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.224) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
home, family, retainers, and dependents. From a historical point of view,
her interactions with Europeans were far fewer than with her immediate
relatives and acquaintances, though no less influential for their brevity.
In 1993, Danilyn Rutherford reviewed the copious Kartini literature
in "Unpacking A National Heroine."89 Advancing understanding of this
observer of Java was the transcription in full of all Kartini's handwritten
letters to members of the Abendanon family and their preparation for pub­
lication by the late Fritz Jacquet in 1987.90 These letters provided answers
to questions vexing the Kartini scholars, such as details concerning her
parentage. Most important for me was the extensive commentary Kartini
made on Java in the years 1900-1903. 91 Joost Cote extended Jacquet's work
by translating and publishing the complete text of her letters to Stella Zee­
handelaar. 92
New material for the study of the Netherlands Indies in the years of
competing nationalisms can be found in Cote's 1998 article on the letters
Kartini's younger sisters wrote to the Abendanons following her death and
until 1936.93 Cote's edition of them in English under the title Realizing the
Dream of R. A. Kartini, his extensive annotations, and his commentary
represent a major contribution to the intellectual history of modern In­
donesia. 94 Mia Bustam's translation of a selection of these letters from the
original Dutch into Indonesian ensures an Indonesian readership for this
graphic record of rapid social and political change in the last decades of
colonial rule.95
Kartini is an ambiguous heroine for many Indonesians. Her intimate
friendships with Dutch people, her ambition to be a single career woman,
and her denunciation of polygamy and child marriage did not find sym­
pathy from nationalists and Islamists, either in the years immediately fol­
lowing her death or now. Another disconcerting figure from colonial times
is the artist Raden Saleh (1807-1880). His paintings and life have been
the subject of several studies by Indonesian authors,96 but a definitive bi­
ography remains to be written. How Saleh negotiated the twin worlds of
colonial and Javanese elites remains to be explored. For example, through
what form did he marry the Christian Eurasian Anna Winkelman? Did she
lose her European status on marriage to a Javanese Muslim? How did Saleh
arrange a divorce from her and subsequent marriage into the extended
Yogyakarta royal family? Can his paintings be interpreted as subtle anti­
colonial, "proto-nationalist" statements? Did he "orientalize" himself as
an Eastern prince in the petty courts of central Europe?
During the same period there were Dutchmen in the colony "orientaliz­
ing" themselves. Theosophists worshipped an ancient Java of their imagin­
ing and pursued studies of Sanskrit and old Javanese literature; they wore
robes and renounced eating meat. Dutch and Javanese of similar affinities
192

--- PAGE: 225 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.225) ---
NEW EXPLORATIONS OF EUROPEAN-ASIAN ENCOUNTERS
met for study and socializing in theosophical societies. They supported the
Java Institute, founded in 1919 to further philological studies, collect and
collate manuscripts, and publish Java's literary heritage. They promoted
knowledge of Javanese culture through support for Yogyakarta's Sonobu­
doyo Museum, which was established in 1935. Admiration for a pre-Dutch
past found political expression in the notion that a people capable of self­
rule must reconnect with their roots. Theosophists were comfortable in
considering Islam a foreign religion that had weakened Javanese culture
and thus helped create conditions that led to Javanese backwardness in
comparison to Dutch dynamism.
Iskandar P. Nugraha reveals the substantial support theosophists gave
to Indonesian students in a master's thesis titled "The Theosophical Edu­
cational Movement in Colonial Indonesia."97 Membership subscriptions
of the Theosophical Society supported the studies in Java and in Holland
of a number of Indonesians who were to become prominent in the na­
tionalist movement. Mohammad Hatta, Indonesia's first vice-president,
for example, received support from the colony's theosophists for university
studies in Holland. Photographs in the KITLV archive show beneficiaries
of the theosophists in Holland, the men dapper in suits, women in frocks
and bobbed hair. Rudolf Mrazek uses the term "Indonesian dandy" for the
men. He explored newspapers, magazines, catalogues, photographs, and
fiction for his study of the Indonesian variant of the dandy and the politics
of clothing. "People were struggling," he writes, "to find outfits through
which they could make a statement about the modern age."98
Kees van Dijk's study of soap in the colony complements this study,
for he constructs a history of hygiene that incorporated Indies inhabitants
into new modes of modernity. New products and advertisements for them
altered personal habits, created new attitudes to the body, one's own and
others', and entangled purchasers in consumption patterns engendered by
advertising. 99 The KITLV images archive has again proved for me an im­
portant source of information and ideas for a study of bathing and hygiene
practices and of inter-community relations. lOo
There were other Indonesians untouched by these trends. They rode
the colonists' trains through a Javanese landscape remade by commer­
cial plantation agriculture to pursue quite different ambitions. Ann Ku­
mar has followed one Mas Rahmat, who documented, in Malay written
in the modified Arabic script called jawi, travels through Java to meditate
in caves, receive those who acknowledged his special powers to heal and
foretell the future, deliver opinions on correct religious practice, and medi­
ate conflicts.WI Mas Rahmat was a well-to-do landowner, had connections
to the Yogyakarta royal family, and had received a solid pesantren (Islamic
school) education. It was the 1880s, a highpoint of colonial rule, when the

--- PAGE: 227 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.227) ---
NEW EXPLORATIONS OF EUROPEAN-ASIAN ENCOUNTERS
grip of the foreigner on the Indonesian archipelago was strong. And yet
the Dutch barely enter the consciousness of the diarist. Rahmat lived in a
parallel thought world, even as he assimilated into his lifestyle eating with
knife and fork and comparing the living standard of imams with that of
high Dutch officials. The material culture and transformations to Javanese
lifestyles that the cash economy brought and that engulfed Javanese such
as Rahmat are detailed in data collected from 2,900 households distributed
among 24 villages in the Comal district of East Java at the end of the nine­
teenth century and analyzed by a group of scholars in Beneath the Smoke
of the Sugar-Mill. 102
Dutch scholarly techniques of textual criticism turned some Javanese
to the past. Alan Feinstein, discussing preservation of manuscripts in In­
donesia, notes the importance of sustaining the interest of future genera­
tions in their literary heritages. These are documented in Illuminations,
the beautifully illustrated study of the writing traditions of Indonesia's
peoples. 103 But many Indonesians, in the last decades of colonial rule, no
longer sought inspiration in chronicles of former kings. They began to
think about the future in political terms derived from study of European
thinkers. They wanted separation from the weight of the past, Indigenous
and foreign, and to develop a new, Indonesian identity and a people that
would stand alone.
The tools of modernity, such as mechanized transport, roads, telegraph,
newspapers, postal service, and radio, encouraged and facilitated a simi­
lar separation among the Dutch. Frances Gouda explored Dutch culture
overseas through prisms of colonial rhetoric, exhibition, and practice in
the years 1900-1942. 104 Immigrants could live differently, more connected
to Holland in mentality than before. Elsbeth Locher-Scholten charts the
outer signs of this change in "Summer Dresses and Canned Food."105
Henk Schulte Nordholt calls this phenomenon "Tropical Netherland" in
contrast to the Indies of Tempo Doeloe. The letters his own mother wrote
from 1937 to her mother-in-law in Holland typify how Headrick's "tools
of empire" transformed Dutch life styles.106 In these letters written from
the fringes of the colony, from Sumbawa, Flores, and Timor, Oet Schulte
Nordholt-Zielhuis gave accounts of the Dutch-language sermon broadcast
over the radio each Sunday, the cost of fruit in the market, and hanging
new curtains. She mentioned in passing her visits paid to the ladies of lead­
ing local families, but detail was reserved for things, thoughts, and actions
she shared in common with her relative in Zwolle.
Here we see the dissolution of the Mestizo world from the Dutch
side, the commitment to remain Dutch. In the letters from Oet Schulte
Nordholt-Zielhuis's near contemporary, Soematri Sosrohadikoesoemo,
written between 1901 and 1936, we see the same pulling apart, the rejection
195

--- PAGE: 228 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.228) ---
THE SOCIAL WORLD OF BATAVIA
by elite Javanese of the in-between person, the Eurasian and Mestizo cul­
ture. Instead, we find personal attachment of Javanese to individual Euro­
peans but the sense that husbands, at least, must make their way against all
career obstacles, be they Eurasian or Dutch. 107
And for the Eurasian? Ulbe Bosma and Remco Raben take a long view
in Being "Dutch" in the Indies. I08 They use the term Indische (Indies) to
underline that Eurasians, Creoles, and long-term European residents in
the Indies partook of a broad culture and orientation. Indische was not
a racial classification, although being of mixed ancestry and heritage was
a major component. Bosma and Raben narrate and analyze what I called
Mestizo history from the "inside." They stress that over time Eurasians and
long-term residents became part of the many regional societies in which
they lived. The authors substantiate their argument through biographies
of Indische families. What stands out in this study is that the lives explored
and retold are not just from Java, but from across the archipelago. We en­
counter the Messman family, whose members were knit into Bugis families
and histories, and the Neijs of Ternate, as well as the Dezentje clan, who
were part of the entourage of the Surakarta principality. Sinjo Frederik, a
Eurasian of Surabaya, became known as Pangeran Timoer and a devout
Muslim. Other lives can only be documented as members of groups, such
as the Eurasian children in orphanages and military barracks.
Bosma and Raben argue that in the years leading up to World War II
the impact of American motion pictures was pre-eminent among the many
influences drawing Indische people more firmly into the European commu­
nity. This "in-between" group became more Westernized, rather than more
"Dutchified." And so, when the politics of nationalism brought an end of
Mestizo society, those opting for a future as Europeans dispersed across
the world. Memoirs of a lost homeland, bitter memories of the terrors of
Indonesia in revolution, the failed vision of a new homeland in West New
Guinea, and the golden age conjured up by Tempo Doeloe remain for the
Indische while Europeans and Asians pursue new encounters and relation­
ships in the twenty-first century.I09

Appendix 3
Family and Position in VOC Batavia
Political office and family alliance linked members of the VOC elite. While there was in
VOC Batavia no dynastic system along patriarchal lines, candidates for high office came
from a pool of men who were connected by marriage to Indies women. Governor­
General P. A. van der Parra's Council exemplified this web of office and family relation­
ship. Uncle and nephew, father-in-law and son-in-law sat on his Council in 1775. Such
links can be established between twelve of the thirteen members. Only two members
were Asia-born, one being the governor-general himself. To van der Parra belongs the
further distinction of being the .only head of Holland's Asian possessions who never saw
Europe. The others confonn to the usual pattern as immigrants, manying locally, keep­
ing daughters in Asia as brides for their colleagues, and sending their sons to Europe.
The Council of the Indies in 1775 consisted of Govemor-Geneml Petrus Albertus van
der Parra, Reynier de Klerk, Willem Arnold Alting, Hendrik Breton, Johan Vos, Willem
Fockens, Thomas Schippers, Jacobus Johannes Craan, David Johan Smith, J. C. M.
Radermacher, Hendrik van Stockum, Jan Hendrik Poock (or Pook), and First Councillor
and Director-General of Trade Jeremias van Riemsdijk. Links between Council members
will be outlined below.
1. PETRUS ALBERTUS van der PARRA (governor-general 1761-75) was born in
Ceylon in 1714, the son of Cornelis Romboutsz. van der Parra, secretary of Ceylon's
government, and of the Dutch-born Geertroida Susanna Spannijt. His paternal grand­
father was Rombout van der Parra, himself a VOC employee on Ceylon and husband of
the Ceylonese-born Henriette Wichelman. Her father, Magnus Wichelman, had served
the Company in Ceylon and Malabar, and finally as director of Persia.
P. A. van der Parra's mother remarried the Netherlander Adriaan Maten in 1721.
Maten was administrator of Colombo by 1729, and served as commander of Malabar
during the years 1731-34.
Van der Parra married twice. His second marriage was to Adriana Johanna Bake,
widow of the VOC commander Anthonij Guldenann. Her father was David Johan Bake,
who had migrated from Holland in 1718 with the rank of junior merchant. Most of
Bake's working life was spent in the Moluccas and included governorship of Ambon
(1732-37). He died in Batavia in 1738 with the rank of councillor extraordinary ~
Bake's wife and the governor-geneml's mother-in-law was Ida Dudde, daughter of a
Batavian burgher and widow of a low-level VOC employee, Constantijn Coomans. A
daughter from her first marriage, Agatha Geertroida Coomans, married the Batavia VOC
225

--- PAGE: 258 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.258) ---
APPENDIX 3: FAMILY AND POSITION IN VOC BATAVIA
employee Johan Smith, and their son, DAVID SMITH, sat on van der Parra's Council in
1775. Another daughter, Ida Constantia Coomans, married Gemt Mom, and their
daughter was grandmother to the future governor-general A. H. Wiese (1805-11).
Ida Dudde's son from her second marriage, Willem Jacob Bake, had a daughter
christened Ida Wilhelmina. She married Johan Frederik baron van Reede tot de Parkeler,
whose first wife had been a daughter of Councillor JOHAN VOS.
The marriage of P. A. van der Parra and Adriana Bake produced only one child, a son
born in 1760 and known as Petrus Albertus van der Parra de jonge. He died in 1783, but
by that time was already chief fiscal officer of the Indies and husband of Catharina
Geertruida Breton, daughter of Councillor HENDRIK BRETON.
2. REYNIER de KLERK sailed to the Indies as a ship's boy in 1710. On his third
voyage he reached Batavia in December 1730 and entered civilian service as a book­
keeper. His career took him to south Sumatra, Surabaya, Semarang, and Banda. His
appointment as councillor extraordinary came in 1754, followed by full councillor in
1762, director-general, and then governor-general from 1777 until his death in office in
1780.
The same year as his appointment as councillor extraordinary, de Klerk married
Sophia Francina Westpalm in Batavia. She was the daughter of Michiel Westpalm, who
was first councillor and director-general under Governor-General Dirk van Cloon (1732­
35), and of Geertmida Margaretha Goossens. Geertruida Goossens was the daughter of
the Batavia VOC merchant Johannes Goossens and of Sophia Fauconier. Following
Goossens's death, Sophia Fauconier remarried Gaspar van Mansdale, who was nomi­
nated councillor extraordinary in 1723. Geertruida Goossens married, in order, Michiel
Westpalm and Johannes Thedens, who was acting governor-general from 1741 to 1743.
In 1736 she had been betrothed to Frederik Julius Coyett, grandson of the governor of
Ceylon and son of the governor of Banda and Ambon. She was, too, godmother to the
children of Governor-General Jacob Mossel.
A sister of Sophia Westpalm, Geertruida Johanna Westpalm, married the Dutch
lawyer Mr. Johan Hendrik van Panhuys. Their son, Johan Michiel, was head of the
Japara residency in the 17808.
Sophia Westpalm (1722-85) had been married to Councillor Hugo Verijssel. One of
the three children from this marriage, Margaretha Sophia Verijssel, was the first wife of
the J. C. M. RADERMACHER who sat on the Council in 1775. Radermacher's second
wife connected him to DAVID SMITH, as will be outlined below.
De Klerk had no legitimate issue. He was buried in 1780 in the family mausoleum of
Hugo Verijssel. His widow recognized Radermacher's son, Frans Reinier, as her heir.
3. WILLEM ARNOLD ALTING went out in 1750 with the rank of junior merchant
and climbed the ranks of the VOC hierarchy in Batavia. He was made a councillor
extraordinary by 1759 and full councillor in 1772. He was the senior member under
Governor-General R. de Klerk and was governor-general from 1780 to 1797, another
period of family government marked by alliances, particularly between the Alting, van
Riemsdijk, and Senn van Basel families.
Alting had ten children by his first wife. Five survived to adulthood, all girls, and all
married native Dutchmen who held posts as senior merchants and residents of Java
226

--- PAGE: 259 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.259) ---
APPENDIX 3: FAMILY AND POSITION IN VOC BATAVIA
districts. The most prominent was the second husband of Alting' s daughter, Pieternella
Gerhardina, who married Mr. Joannes Siberg. Siberg was to follow his father-in-law as
governor-general during the years 1801-5.
Alting's second wife was Maria Susanna Grebel, whose first husband was Councillor
Huybert van Basel, patriarch of the Senn van Basel clan. Alting' s stepdaughter, Maria
Wilhelmina Senn van Basel (1770-1821), married his nephew Nicolaas Engelhard,
governor of the North Coast of Java and later a councillor. Another relative of Alting' s,
Pieter Engelhard, was to many a daughter of Councillor Extraordinary Anthonij Barkeij
and later a van Riemsdijk, which brought further links to the Senn van Basel clan also.
4. HENDRIK BRETON was married to Sara Maria van Oordt. During his tenn as
resident of Surabaya a daughter, Catharina Geertmida, was born. She was to many P. A.
van der Parra de jonge in Batavia in 1778. Their descendants were very numerous in the
nineteenth century. Breton was first councillor and director-general under GG Alting
until his death in office in 1780.
5. JORAN VOS held among other posts those of senior merchant and governor of
Java's North Coast {1765-7 1), and was appointed full councillor in 1777. His first wife
connected him to the governor-general's family, for she was Adriana Agatha Smith,
sister of DAVID SMITH. They had married in 1753. She died sometime before 1762,
when Vos married Ida Wilhelmina Bake, niece of Governor-General van der PARRA.
His daughter, Ida Petronella Jacoba Vos, married F. H. van Reede tot de Parkeler, as
noted above.
6. WILLEM FOCKENS served as councillor extraordinary and full councillor during
van der Parra's governor-generalship. He died in office in 1780. His wife, Catharina
Tobison, was to remany fellow councillor DAVID SMITH.
7. THOMAS SCHIPPERS went out to the Indies in 1740 as a junior merchant. His
career took him to Bantam and Malacca, where he was governor over the years 1764-72.
In the latter year he was appointed councillor extraordinary and president of the court in
Batavia. He became a full councillor in 1778 and died in Batavia in 1780.
Schippers's third wife was Catharina Cornelia van Mijlendonck, widow of Councillor
Hendrik van Ossenbergh and daughter of Johan Elias van Mijlendonck, governor of
Ternate 1755-56 and councillor extraordinary. This marriage connected Schippers to the
Reynst family, whose members were numerous in the Indies.
8. JACOBUS JOHANNES CRAAN was born in Ambon in 1728, son of the Dutch
minister Petrus Craan. He joined the VOC's service as a clerk in 1741 and worked his
way up in Malacca and on Java until his appointment as councillor extraordinary. This
was followed by full councillor in 1780, the year of his death in Batavia.
Craan was an uncle of HENDRIK BRETON. He married the fifteen-year-old Johanna
Henrietta Breekpot in 1753. She was the daughter of Comelis Breekpot, commander of
Malabar during the period 1764-69. From Craan's marriage to Johanna Breekpot was
born Catharina Margaretha Craan, who was to many the son of JEREMIAS van
227

--- PAGE: 260 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.260) ---
APPENDIX 3: FAMILY AND POSITION IN VOC BATAVIA
RIEMSDIJK in 1773 and be the mother of fourteen of his children. The children from the
van Riemsdijk -eraan alliance are listed in appendix 1, table 9.
9. DAVID JOHAN SMITH, as noted in section 1, was the son of Governor-General
van der PARRA's wife's half-sister, Agatha G. Smith-Coomans. He was sent in 1745 at
age five to the Netherlands for schooling on the request of his maternal grandmother, Ida
Dudde. He returned in 1762, and profited from his uncle's position as governor-general,
being promoted from the rank of junior to senior merchant without the intermediate post
of merchant. At thirty-two he was made councillor extraordinary, and full councillor by
1781. By 1789 he was senior councillor and due to be appointed second-in-command as
director-general, but withdrew from government service and died on his estate in 1792.
Smith manied four times. His first wife (1763) was Hester Petronella Romp, daughter
of Ordinary Councillor Michiel Romp and sister of the wife of the future councillor
extraordinary Johan van der Burgh. One of their children, Maria Petronella Smith, was
to marry Jacobus Theodorus Reynst, which allied the family to the van Mijlendonck and
SCHIPPER's clans.
Smith's second wife was Catharina Tobison, widow ofWll..LEM FOCKENS.
The third wife was Catharina Louisa Gallas, mother, through a previous marriage, of
Anna Jacoba Bosch, who was the second wife of J. C. M. RADERMACHER. Catharina
died in 1782, and Smith had his stepdaughter Anna placed in van der PARRA's house­
hold until her marriage at thirteen to Radermacher.
Smith's fourth marriage lasted longest. It took place in 1782 and was to Johanna
Mauritia Mohr, daughter of the well-known Batavia clergyman and astronomer and of
Anna Elisabeth van't Hoff.
Finally, Smith was designated guardian of P. A. van der Parra de jonge in the will the
governor-general had drawn up in 1773.
10. J. C. M. RADERMACHER has already been recorded as son-in-law of councillor
and future governor-general de KLERK. Radermacher had first gone out to the Indies at
sixteen as a junior merchant. His marriage to Margaretha Verijssel took place in Batavia
in 1761, when de Klerk was already councillor extraordinary. In 1763 Radermacher
returned on furlough to the Netherlands. Margaretha accompanied him and probably died
in the Netherlands. The only child of the marriage, Frans Reinier Radermacher, was
named Sophia de Klerk -Westpalm' s heir.
After his return to Batavia in 1767 Radermacher was appointed councillor extra­
ordinary (1776), and full councillor one year later. His second wife, Anna Jacoba Bosch,
has already been noted as DAVID SMITH's stepdaughter. She accompanied Rader­
macher when he repatriated in 1783, but remained at the Cape after his murder at sea,
and was to remarry there Christiaan Anthonie Ver Huell, son of the Indies traveler and
chronicler. Anna Radermacher-Bosch' s sisters were married to men who headed resi­
dencies on Java.
11. HENDRIK van ST<X:KUM went out as ship's boy in 1754 and transferred into
the civilian ann of the VOC as a junior assistant in Batavia. By 1768 he was made junior
merchant and had climbed the hierarchy to first senior merchant of the Castle by 1771.
His appointment as councillor extraordinary came in 1776, followed by that of full

councillor in 1786. Three years later he was senior councillor and director-general, and
died in Batavia in 1793.
Van Stockum did not marry until 1779. His bride was Catharina Elisabeth Roms­
winckel, widow of Otto Frans Nicolaas Marci, who had been in charge of artillery and
warehouses for the VOC. A daughter from this first marriage, Christina Elisabeth Marci,
was to many the future governor-general A. H. Wiese. From the van Stockum-Roms­

Romswinckel at Batavia in 1801. He had been born in the Netherlands, but emigrated
and died in Batavia in 1846 after a career culminating in appointment as councillor
extraordinary.
As noted above, the Wiese family was connected with the Senn van Basel, van
Riemsdijk, and Alting families as well as with the Romswinckel family. Wiese's sister
Ida Adriana Helena married Willem Adriaan Senn van Basel, who was half-brother of
Maria Wilhelmina Engelhard, step-daughter of Governor-General Alting. W A. Senn
van Basel's second wife was a legitimate daughter of W V. H. van Riemsdijk, while his
sister, Philippina Maria Theodora Senn van Basel, had married Steven Poelman, widow
of GG Alting's oldest daughter, Elisabeth Fockleyna Alting.
12. JAN HENDRIK POOCK was the VOC's bailiff in Batavia in 1774 and councillor
extraordinary under Governors-General van der Parra, van Riemsdijk, de Klerk, and
Alting. I have not established any family connections of this council member yet.
13. JEREMIAS van RIEMSDIJK first went out to the Indies in 1735 as a sergeant in
the VOC army. He was the nephew of Governor-General Adriaan Valckenier (1737-41).
Valckenier's first wife, Johanna Alida Tolling, was aunt of Jeremias van Riemsdijk's
fourth wife, Adriana Louisa Helvetius.
1. van Riemsdijk left the army upon arrival in Batavia and, benefiting from his
connections, reached the rank of senior merchant by 1740. He survived Valckenier's
disgrace, and was councillor extraordinary by 1753 and full councillor by 1760. He served
as van der Parra's director-general from 1764 until his election by unanimous vote of the
Council upon van der Parra's death in office in 1775. Van Riemsdijk died in office in 1777.
Van Riemsdijk married five times. His alliances are laid out in appendix 2, table 3.
The most important family connections were made on his fourth and fifth marriages.
Adriana Helvetius's father was president of Batavia's court, while her maternal grand­
father was Councillor Laurens Tolling. One of her uncles was Willem Maurits
Bruininck, governor and director of Ceylon from 1740 to 1742. The fifth wife, Theodora
Rotgers, had been married previously to a member of Batavia's court and then to
Councillor Extraordinary Mr. Maurits Theodorus Hilgers. His brother-in-law Nicolaas
Hartingh had married a daughter of Governor-General Jacob Mosse!.
Van Riemsdijk's daughter Johanna Maria (by his third wife) married Paulus
Godefridus van der Voort in 1765. He rose to be governor and director of Makassar
during the decade 1770-80. His son Willem V. H. van Riemsdijk, who was a councillor,
married Catharina Craan, daughter of Councillor JACOBUS 1. CRAAN. Their daughter
Jacoba Dorothea was the second wife of W A. Senn van Basel and mother of
Wilhelmina Henriette Senn van Basel, who married the future governor-general Jean­
Chretien Baud.

18 Large areas of Java had been transferred to VOC suzerainty in the course of the
previous century. Following on Speelman's successes against Truna Djaja, Krawang,
part of east Priangan, and Semarang had been ceded to Batavia. In 1705 Batavia
gained control over the rest of Priangan and Madura, and over the Surabaya area in
1743. Finally, in 1755, the government under Mossel was able to make permanent a
division of the old Matararn. Henceforth the sultanates of Solo and Yogyakarta were
known to the Dutch as the Vorstenlanden, the so-called independently ruled prince­
doms.

CHAPTER 4: THE ASSAULT ON INDIES CULTURE
On van Imhoff as governor of Ceylon and in particular his attention to reforming the
schooling provided by the Company to local boys of European, Tamil, and Singhal­
ese origin, see Jurrien van Goor, Jan Kompenie as Schoolmaster: Dutch Education in
Ceylon i690-/795 (Groningen: Wolters-Noordhoff, 1978), chap. 4. The VOC
played a far greater role in promoting Western education in Ceylon than in its other
settlements, including its capital, and sent numbers of seminarians for theological
studies in the Netherlands. Some were later to serve in parishes in Dutch Asia,
including Batavia.

The Bataviasche Courant (Batavian Journal) was published by the government press
in the capital. Its first issue appeared on 20 August 1816. In time its name was
changed to lavasche Courant in recognition of the island-wide community it served,
and it became a daily paper.

--- PAGE: 291 (Source: 2000-Taylor-The_Social_World_of_Batavia_Europeans_Eurasians.pdf p.291) ---
Selected Bibliography
Note: The authorities on which this study is founded are generally acknowledged in
footnotes to the text. The sources for the family histories are not usually cited, however,
since the relationships described have been detennined by drawing on various studies of
several authors. The following note outlines the nature of the material used to establish
family trees.
The principal sources used to establish Indies clans are the Dutch scholars P. C. Bloys
van Treslong Prins, P. R. Feith, F. de Haan, M. A. van Rhede van der Kloot, and W.
Wijnaendts van Resandt. Bloys van Treslong Prins collected texts from tombstones of
the European community on Java and began publishing them in 1934. The epitaphs are
grouped by geographic region, but there is an index of surnames. In collaboration with P.
R. Feith, the same author published the complete genealogical table for the van Riems­
dijken down to the 19208, a task commissioned for testamentary purposes. Wijnaendts
van Resandt published summaries of the careers of all heads of settlements subsidiary to
Batavia, in which he gave the names of spouses and children, and appended a register of
family names. The posts of senior merchant and governor were common stepping stones
to higher office, so that his work ties in with similar compilations by F. de Haan for
officials in Priangan and by van Rhede van der Kloot for the governors-general.
Additional sources were L. Ludovici' s collection of Dutch tombstone inscriptions
from Ceylon, Steinmetz's notes on the texts on china and memorial trays that summarize
family alliances, and notes in Dutch genealogical journals.
The sources I have used dictated that the family histories discovered be those of local
elites. The same reasons of worldly distinction made the senior officials and their fami­
lies the subjects of paintings, odes, descriptions by travelers, and legal regulations, and
again more accessible for study than the soldier and sailor.
The bibliography that follows is divided into seven sections for ease of reference. All
titles in foreign languages are also given in English in my translation. Authors' names are
listed according to Dutch practice.
DICfIONARIES, BffiLIOGRAPHIES, ENCYCWPEDIAS

ments from Netherlands India/Indonesia). Leiden: Koninklijk Instituut voor Taal-,
Land- en Volkenkunde, 1973.
